   1|                                                PAUL B. PORTER - Paul was born in Fitchburg and grew up in Lunenburg and
   2|                                                graduated from Lunenburg High School. Following his graduation, Paul joined the U.S. Marine Corps and served for four years. He was honorably discharged in 1963 having
   3|                                                attained the rank of corporal. When he returned home he became a reserve police officer
   4|                                                with the Lunenburg Police Department and served in that capacity for 46 years.
   5|                                                Paul was a member of the Lunenburg Planning Board and Zoning Board of Appeals. He
   6|                                                was president of the Lunenburg Historical Society, member of the Masons and served as a
   7|                                                Constable. He was member and past president of the Lunenburg Police Relief Association and senior vice commander of the American Legion Post 283.
   8|                     Paul was a proud Marine Corps Devil Dog and member and past Commandant of the Marine Corps League Fitchburg
   9|                     Detachment #1076. He was honored by the Department of Massachusetts Marine Corps League as their "Marine of the
  10|                     Year" for 2011-2012.
  11|                     knew him loved him. He was affectionately referred to as "The Mayor of Lunenburg" by his daughter Pam and many others. Everyone who
  12|                                                 RICHARD "NICK" STORRS - Nick was born and brought up in New
  13|                                                 York, educated at Pomfret School and Yale University, and moved to Fitchburg in 1962 to He was
  14|                                                 teach  at Applewild School after teaching in Switzerland for two years.
  15|                                                 instrumental in forming the Fitchburg Youth Hockey League which played at Applewild's
  16|                                                  outdoor rink before the Wallace Civic Center was built in the early 1970s.
  17|                                                 Nick moved to Lancaster Avenue in Lunenburg in 1970 and quickly became involved with
  18|                                                 town activities. Whether it was sports or additional education programs he was always
  19|                                                 ready to volunteer for something. He introduced the Math Olympiad program to the 5ih
  20|                                                 and fth grades in the early 1980s and taught the kids the importance of thinking through a
  21|                                                 word problem rather than just concentrating on the "right" answer. Nick served on the
  22|                      School Committee for several years and became the Chairman in 1988. Always concerned about the children of
  23|                     Lunenburg getting the best education available, he worked closely with the Superintendents and other members of the
  24|                      School Committee toward that goal.
  25|                     In 1988 Nick became a Realtor with Century 21 Fontaine in Fitchburg and subsequently bought the franchise. He and
  26|                     his wife, Jeanie, created Century 21 Realty Team and moved the business to Leominster. Covering most of the towns
  27|                     of Northern Worcester County, the philosophy of the company has always focused on service and serving the real
  28|                     estate needs of the community. As a very active member of the Montachusett Area Rotary Club Nick's enthusiasm for
  29|                     "Service Above Self" extended to both local civic projects as well as multiple trips to Central America with Habitat for
  30|                      Humanity. At home, he became a Commissioner of Trust Funds and served as Chairman until his death in 2012

===PAGE 9=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                Sullivan as our new Fire Chief.
   2|                In 2012, the Town formed a School Building Committee to continue working toward the goal of building a new or renovate
   3|                an existing school building. Please review the Superintendent of School's report for additional information.
   4|                A good portion of the first half of 2012 was dedicated to reviewing the FY2013 Budget proposal and Override request. After
   5|                a significant amount of discussion, the Board of Selectmen, School Committee and Finance Committee agreed that it was
   6|                time to ask the community to consider an Override. The impact of the $2.2M Operating Override to the Average
   7|                Homeowner was determined to be just under $500 per year. In the end, the Override was defeated by a large margin, and
   8|                as a result, several large scale changes were made, including the decision to consolidate four schools into three and to
   9|                transition all Administrative and Land Use Offices to a 4-day work schedule.
  10|                A significant amount of time was spent this past year reviewing three different, large solar projects: Borrego at the Town's
  11|                Landfill; EPG at Pleasant & West Streets; and Mass PV1 on Chase Road. The project at the Town's Landfill site is a town-
  12|                sponsored project, while the other two are private projects. Unfortunately, the project at the Landfill was determined to be
  13|                uneconomic. The two private projects are still viable. Both private projects will provide the Town with the opportunity to
  14|                enter into Power Purchase Agreements, whereby the Town can purchase Net Metering Credits as a discounted price to be
  15|                used to offset the Town's electric utility costs for municipal buildings.
  16|                This year, we saw the sale of a town-owned historic building to a private party. The Jones House- the former homestead of
  17|                American botanist, horticulturalist and pioneer in agricultural science Luther Burbank, was sold in June to Larry & Dawn
  18|                Marshall. Under the conditions of sale, Mr. & Mrs. Marshall are required to restore the property in keeping with its historical
  19|                significance. The property's restoration will be monitored by the Town's Historical Commission. To date, the Marshall's
  20|                have done an excellent job!
  21|                In June, with the assistance of the Town's Treasurer-Collector, we were able to refund/ refinance our existing 2003 Bond
  22|                Issue, which resulted in a net savings to the Town of $550,000 over the 11 remaining years of the issue. In the process, the
  23|                Town's AA- Bond Rating was affirmed.
  24|                Finally, the Town continues to push forward in the quest to redevelop the Summer Street Corridor. In 2012, Town Meeting
  25|                authorized funding for an Alternatives Analysis. This was completed and the Board of Selectmen agreed upon a redesign
  26|              which includes one travel lane in each direction, with center turn lanes as specific intersections. The next step in the
  27|                process includes bringing the project to 25% design, at which point, it can be included in the region's TIP (Transportation design at with point it can
  28|                Improvement Plan), which will secure grant funding for construction. The Town owes a big thank you to its legislative
  29|                delegation- Senator Flanagan, Representative Benson and Representative DiNatale - who were all instrumental in securing
  30|                the remaining funding for design in the State's Transportation Bond Bill. As a result of Bond Bill and TIP funding, about
  31|                95% of this project's funding will come from sources other than the Lunenburg taxpayer.
  32|                I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their time and many
  33|                talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.
  34|                Respectfully submitted,
  35|                Kerry A. Speidel
  36|                                                                        6

   1|                  Building permits remain steady for additions, remodeling, roofing, siding, window replacement and decks. Several solar:
   2|                panel installations for both residential and commercial buildings were permitted and increasing each year. Apartment
   3|                construction located at Tri Town Landing is moving forward with building number three under construction consisting of
   4|                  thirty-three apartments. The Emerald Place development at the former Whalom Park is moving ahead steadily with all 238
   5|                  building permits issued.
   6|                  building permits issued in 2012 was 290.
   7|                  The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has adopted the 2009 International Residential Code and 2009 International Energy
   8|                  Conservation Code. The 2009 International Building Code is currently in effect, this is the 8th Edition of the Massachusetts
   9|                  Code with amendments.   The new code affects commercial and residential buildings and additions. All contractors are
  10|                  encouraged to review the new compliance regulations of the code.
  11|                  Carbon monoxide detectors are required in every home existing as well as new construction. Homeowners should be
  12|                  aware that inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance, please contact our office to determine what needs to
  13|                  be done for compliance and safety.
  14|                  Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations who bid or
  15|                  perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with
  16|                  the Commonwealth. This office will enforce this provision; however you should ask your contractor about his/her
  17|                  registration. Please contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call for any information regarding
  18|                 zoning requirements or building code issues.
  19|                                                        CAPITAL PLANNING TOWN REPORT
  20|                  Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY) Plan and a Capital
  21|                  Improvement Plan. These Plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
  22|                  As noted in the past reports the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis, Cost
  23|                  Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six elements. Not only do they
  24|                  have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria.
  25|                  Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans
  26|                  provides the needed service for safety and protection of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of
  27|                  government.
  28|                  At the Annual Town Meeting May 2012, $464,487.00 was approved for Capital Requests.
  29|                  The following items were presented in the Plan.
  30|                  DEPARTMENT                           ITEM                                 AMOUNT

   1|               The Planning Board, aware of the economy as in their monitoring in 2011, continued to monitor its effect on Lunenburg's
   2|               housing situation in 2012. The office continues receiving many requests for information from the citizenry via personal
   3|               concerns about housing status.
   4|             The number of ANR plans (subdivision of lots for separate building lots) increased to 11 for 2012 from the previous year of.
   5|               five (5). The major large project, Emerald Place at Lake Whalom, continued construction according to the approved plan
   6|               with three different housing styles. They are constructed villas with two (2) units (single floor design) per structure. The
   7|               developer's plans are to construct to completion. The units are occupied as each unit or set of units are completed,
   8|               contracted under "lease to own" agreements.
   9|              Tri Town Landing is under construction for Building Three (3). The 40R project Building One & Two are occupied as rentals
  10|              and all sixty-six (66) units have been rented. Building Three (3) will be rentals as well. The Town has received compliments
  11|              on the construction and management of this project.
  12|              Stone Farm, Massachusetts Avenue is still being constructed to completion. Highfield Village, Northfield Road is still in
  13|              review. Several extensions have been given. The project entitled Whitetail Crossing has submitted its "As Built" and plans
  14|              on requesting Town acceptance of the roadway interior. Sequoia Drive and Whites Woods Phase Ill are building upon
  15|              request.
  16|              The Open Space Plan, under the auspices of the Planning Board and Conservation Commission, was reported previously
  17|            as approved by the State. The Ad Hoc Committee for that Plan continues to work, as recommended, to address
  18|              implementation. In 2011 the Committee started to look into an important element in our community; the farming
  19|              community. They began researching the possibility of recommending an Agricultural Commission and the creation of a
  20|              interest. They are working with a State Consultant. "Right to Farm" bylaw. This work continued in 2012 and will involve the farming community. Many farmers have shown
  21|              Work began in 2012 to update the Master Plan and Zoning Bylaw. In addition to the two Zoning Bylaws approved at the
  22|              December 2011 Special Town Meeting; 1) Design Standards Bylaw, and, 2) Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District
  23|              Bylaw, both of which have been approved by the Attorney General's office, the Board has continued working with MRPC
  24|              and the local Board of Selectmen regarding the revitalization of Summer Street. This project will be a complete redesign of Summer Street.
  25|              In addition, the Planning Office worked with MRPC on a DLTA Grant (District Local Technical Assistance) which content
  26|              includes data on employment, population characteristics, economic sector/local tax base, education, brownfields, Chapter
  27|              43D sites, transportation, and environmental and development characteristics of land by zoning. All the above data are the
  28|              background for updating the Master Plan. A public presentation was held for the Town's information. The Board plans on
  29|              continuing work with MRPC in this direction and will be able to apply for the next round. The two issues noted by the
  30|              Board are economics and center planning.
  31|              In 2012 the major planning issue was of renewable energy. The Planning Board worked on permitting three solar projects.
  32|              The three projects were the Town's landfill, 651 Chase Road and 134 Electric Avenue. The projects also went under a
  33|              Development Plan Review. These applications prompted the Planning Board to review the Town's 2009 solar bylaw. An
  34|              updated bylaw was written and passed at the May 2012 Town Meeting. As of this report, the projects are in discussion with
  35|             the Board of Selectmen concerning the method of payment; taxes versus a PILOT program.

   1|                  As a reminder, the Board of Health office has been relocated to the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave :
   2|                  and Leominster Road. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right. We can be reached at 978-345-
   3|                  4146, extension 430.
   4|                  The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to
   5|                  maintain my maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2012, reviewing Title V Inspection Reports,
   6|                 building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public
   7|                 health related issues.
   8|                  Our partnership with the Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project again proved valuable to the Town. Numerous
   9|                 scheduled mosquito sprayings were conducted during the warmer weather period to control the mosquito population
  10|                 within the town. Hundreds of citizens also requested sprayings of their yards. The CMMCP stations mosquito traps for
  11|                 testing around the town and twice discovered insects infected with EEE virus. Sprayings were conducted in those areas as
  12|                 well as a Special spraying of all the school grounds just prior to the Bonfire.
  13|                 Citizens who wish to have their property sprayed for mosquitos should call the Board of Health office who will add you to
  14|                 the schedule. There is no charge for this service.
  15|                 Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other Town Departments and regional
  16|                 entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our region.
  17|                 This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or
  18|                 upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic systems, wells, food
  19|                 service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the environment and
  20|                 groundwaters of the Commonwealth.
  21|                 The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba Associated
  22|                 Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are provided throughout the year to our
  23|                 residents. These clinics provide inoculations, Blood Pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and Dental screening.
  24|                 Fluoride treatment and dental checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  25|                 With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering from illness and
  26|                 Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  27|                 Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  28|                 Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg. If
  29|                you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146 ext.

   1|                                                            HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|                  The Lunenburg Historical Commission was created to identify, safeguard and help preserve for posterity, the unique
   3|                 archeological significance to the Town of Lunenburg physical assets of the Town as exemplified by the various sites, buildings and other edifices of historic, architectural or
   4|                 The Mission Statement above has served to be the guiding principle for the Historical Commission to focus its efforts and
   5|                 follows: work on a number of projects of historical consequence for the Town. The main initiatives implemented this year are as
   6|                 Jones House - The successful conclusion to the Commission's efforts to sell the Jones House at 42 Main Street as a
   7|                 historic property were realized. Mr. & Mrs. Lawrence Matthews purchased the house from the Town and embarked on the
   8|                 process of restoring this significant historic property.
   9|                 Lunenburg Ledger - The program of regular newspaper articles was continued by the Commission to inform Town residents of the unique historical qualities of life in Lunenburg both past and present.
  10|                 Capital Plan - The Commission was consulted this year and we urged that efforts be made in the funding of the Capital
  11|                 Plan to include new paint for the Town Hall along with repairs to the chimneys and the roof.
  12|                 Funding Grant - The Commission sought and was given a $3,000. grant by the Montachusett Society for the Preservation of
  13|                 Historic Buildings to be used for the chimney work at the Town Hall.
  14|                 Demolition Delay Requests - The Commission continued to work closely with the Building Commissioner to review and
  15|                 determine the appropriate course of action for properties seeking demolition permits. During the year multiple properties
  16|                were reviewed and acted upon. The most significant involved a historic property on Leominster-Shirley Road. The
  17|                 Commission has made efforts to make citizens aware of the structure on the property in the hope that it might be relocated.
  18|                 Computerized Database - The development of the Town wide computerized database utilizing the Massachusetts Historical
  19|                 Commission website is an ongoing project. The completion of the web based resource continues to be a main focus of the Commission's long term goals.
  20|                the months of July & August when meetings are on an as needed basis. The Commission meetings are scheduled the first Tuesday of each month at 6:30PM at The Ritter Building except during
  21|                Respectfully submitted,

  24|                                                    INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY DIRECTOR
  25|                The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through the use of technology..
  26|                The ! Department enables all town departments and schools to communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks,
  27|                organize, store, and share information, and generally conduct business more effectively. We also provide the necessary
  28|               infrastructure, equipment, software, and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
  29|               team: The past year has seen some changes in staffing for the Information Technology department. We are now a three person.:
  30|                                                                       15

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 22 lines)
   1|                  Along with serving as helpdesk for all users daily technology issues and the usual maintenance and scheduled
   2|                  replacement of the town's and schools' technology assets, the IT Department has been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year. The following examples are among the more noteworthy improvements that IT has
   3|                implemented.

  15|                                                            FINANCE COMMITTEE
  16|               continue to outpace increases in revenues, we were faced with a very difficult decision: Accept the reduced revenues and 2012 represented a crossroads for the Town of Lunenburg. With decreases in state aid and increases in costs that
  17|               thereby begin dismantling key services provided by the Town or increase revenues in the form of a Proposition 2 ½ override and maintain level services. The Town chose the former by a 2-1 margin at the polls.
  18|               into balance. The balanced budget for FY2013 as approved at Annual Town Meeting required that we limit spending in all areas of town government. With limited ability to increase revenues, we were forced to make dramatic cuts in costs to bring the budget
  19|               In recent years we had taken extraordinary steps to balance the budget, such as furloughs, layoffs, drawing from stabilization to fund unemployment and short term borrowing to fund capital. We do not see this as sustainable. The
  20|               balanced budget put into effect resulted in layoffs, which resulted in costs to fund unemployment. However, we did not draw from stabilization nor did we borrow for capital articles this year because we need to protect our bond rating in
  21|              anticipation of a future school building project. Note that some of the proposed cuts could jeopardize our bond rating in spite of our good intentions.
  22|              As reported in the last several budget letters from this committee, we have been struggling with a structural deficit for 16

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|               We have four sources of revenue to the Town: property tax, state aid, local receipts and available funds. Federal stimulus
   2|               monies which provided temporary additional revenues are no longer available. Increases in property tax are limited by
   3|               Proposition 2 ½: We are not allowed to increase the tax levy by more than 2.5% unless we approve an override. New
   4|               growth is expected to contribute a $350K increase to property tax revenues; however, please note that growth comes with
   5|               additional strain on services and infrastructure. The additional tax we gain from new growth is not enough to offset the
   6|               increase in demand for town services like public safety and education, as well as additional traffic on our roads. State aid
   7|               in the form of Chapter 70 funding for education has increased slightly; however, other forms of state aid have been
   8|               reduced. Local receipts are in decline, with increases in motor vehicle excise tax being offset by decreases in
   9|               supplemental tax and supplemental state aid. Note that state lottery aid has declined by $486K since 2009, or 31%.
  10|               The FY2013 balanced budget funds level service in public safety and painful cuts on other services, such as schools,
  11|               Library, Council on Aging and town offices. To meet the $1.2 million shortfall in education, the school district decided to
  12|               consolidate from four schools into three, moving students out of TC Passios and restructuring the grade configuration at
  13|               the Primary School, Turkey Hill Middle School and Lunenburg High School. Despite this restructuring the School District
  14|               was still forced to eliminate additional teaching positions on top of the 18.5 positions that were eliminated between 2009
  15|               and 2012, resulting in larger class sizes.
  16|               The Council on Aging was faced with a 10% reduction that led to reduced operating hours. At the Library, we were faced
  17|               with a similar dilemma until special Town Meeting last fall, where the Town approved a supplemental appropriation to bring
  18|               the Library budget in line with State guidelines and preserve our eligibility for matching funds and inter-library services
  19|               without the need for a waiver. Note that this appropriation was approved against the recommendations of the Select Board
  20|               and the Finance Committee, who were concerned that additional state funding used for this appropriation could not be
  21|                depended upon. State aid was indeed subsequently reduced.
  22|               Town offices experienced layoffs and services were reduced to a four-day schedule. Again we were not able to provide
  23|               funding for the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan, meaning that we cannot address proper maintenance of our
  24|               roads. The condition of our Town roadways will continue to deteriorate as a result. The Capital Planning Committee
  25|               recommendation for purchases in Technology, School Infrastructure, Public Works, Public Safety and Council on Aging
  26|               was approved and funded without borrowing.
  27|               To summarize, the impact of a balanced budget and failure of the proposed Proposition 2 1/2 override is real and being felt
  28|               virtually every day. The Town made their choice clear by a two to one margin at the annual Town election. The Town
  29|               Manager and Select Board, with the support of the Finance Committee and all the department heads, continue to make the
  30|               challenging decisions to honor the wishes of the Town's people and manage through this difficult time.
  31|                In closing, the Finance Committee wishes to thank all of the dedicated public servants for their hard work and diligence.
  32|             Mark Erickson, Chair                Scott Gile

  20|                                                               FIRE DEPARTMENT
  21|                    The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the .
  22|                      highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
  23|                 As I begin to write my first annual report as your Fire Chief, I find myself reflecting on the changes that have taken place in
  24|                 the Fire Department since I first joined in 1983. In that year, the Fire Department responded to a total of 425 calls for service, with 248 being Emergency Medical Calls. At that time, the Town's population was a little over 8,000 people and the
  25|                 duty during the day. We had no apartment buildings, shopping malls or even traffic lights! Much of the town was still farm Fire Department consisted of a Full Time Chief and 50 on call Firefighters and EMT's. The Chief was the only person on
  26|               • land.
  27|                 Fast forward to today. In 2012 your Fire Department responded to 1698 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  28|                                                                         18

===PAGE 22=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                 volume of calls for assistance continues to trend upward, with no particular ceiling in sight given the ongoing building in
   2|                 town. These factors lead me to continue to call for 24 hour staffing in the Station. Providing funding to staff two
   3|                 Firefighter/EMT's overnight and on weekends would allow either the ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond
   4|                immediately. Reducing the response time by four or more minutes is critical in cases of serious medical emergencies or
   5|                 structure fires. This staffing would in no way reduce the need for our staff of on call personnel; it would only supplement
   6|                 their response and reduce response times. In fact, the current recommendation is to use our existing personnel on a per diem basis to provide the staffing.
   7|                 Our firefighters continue to train for a variety of situations which we can encounter even in a town such as ours. Today's
   8|                firefighter must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care, hazardous materials mitigation and technical rescue.
   9|                They must be familiar with hazards which didn't exist even a few years ago, such as hybrid fueled vehicles, lightweight
  10|                building construction, domestic terrorism and clandestine drug labs, just to name a few. Our EMT's must continue to stay
  11|                abreast of the latest treatment protocols and advances in lifesaving skills. Our members train regularly both here at the
  12|                Station and at classes sponsored by other agencies. While they are compensated for some of the training, the vast
  13|                majority is done on their own time. To give a snapshot of some of the training required today, a new Firefighter/EMT must
  14|                attend over 300 hours of basic firefighting training at the Massachusetts Fire Academy. In addition, they must complete
  15|                another 140 hours of training as an Emergency Medical Technician. Once they are certified, they must complete over 50
  16|                hours of continuing education and refresher training every two years, just for their EMT. Operators of Fire Apparatus must
  17|                complete an additional 65 hours of training in pump and aerial operations, hydraulics calculations and vehicle operation.
  18|                Again, these requirements are for call personnel, and most of this is done on their own time.
  19|                Several of our personnel continue to maintain skills in specialized Technical Rescue, such as Cave In, Confined Space,
  20|             •. Rope and Underwater Search and Recovery. These members are part of two regional teams which provide those services not only to Lunenburg, but the whole North County area.
  21|                Fire Prevention activities are a major part of the Department's activity. Personnel must review and inspect all new
  22|                construction in town for proper fire protection. In addition, there are inspections which are required by law such as Smoke
  23|               and Carbon Monoxide Detector Installations when a home is sold and oil burner and propane gas installations. In addition
  24|               any business holding a liquor license must be inspected each year for fire safety issues as well. Personnel also continue
  25|               to provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well. Several of our members also provide first aid and CPR training to both citizens and town employees.
  26|               The transition of our Communications Center to the Nashoba Valley Dispatch Center is continuing with an anticipated
  27|               switch over in June 2013. This center will handle all of the 911 calls and public safety communications for our town, Devens, Harvard and Lancaster.
  28|               Several Federal Grants have been applied for and were received or are currently pending. These grants will hopefully
  29|            provided much needed training and equipment for our personnel. We continue to look for alternative sources of funding to support the services we provide.
  30|               The rebuild of Engine 2 (our light brush truck) which was funded by Town Meeting in 2011 was completed in March. The ..
  31|               unit's body was replaced with a lighter, more efficient body, pump and tank design. In addition, upgrades and repairs were made to the existing 1989 chassis. This will allow the truck to be much more efficient off road and should add several
  32|              years to its lifespan.
  33|               Town Meeting also provided funds to perform a partial overhaul of Engine 4. This unit, a 1995 pumper has been our
  34|               workhorse since its arrival and was in need of significant work. At the time of writing, the truck's pump has been
  35|               overhauled and repairs to the body corrosion are scheduled. These repairs, in addition to others will hopefully extend this 19

===PAGE 26=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                In January, our hopes of a much need renovation of the Public Works facility were realized. The existing building at 520.
   2|                 Chase Rd. was totally renovated inclusive of new office space, efficient and safe heating/cooling and ventilation. The
   3|                installation of more efficient lighting and numerous energy saving upgrades were rewarded with a $14,000.00 rebate from
   4|                our electrical supplier. Additionally, a separate structure was erected which allows for our entire fleet to be under cover.
   5|                This has, as expected, decreased response time during winter events. Over time, the protection of our vehicles from severe
   6|                weather will prove invaluable. Modernized office space allows for a professional working environment for staff and
   7|                residents requiring service.
   8|                 It is impossible to express the level of appreciation to you the residents who supported the project and especially those
   9|                individuals who gave so much and worked so diligently toward the success of this project. Thank you.
  10|                PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  11|                Consistent with the prioritization as determined by our Pavement Management Plan, road improvements were initiated on a
  12|                portion of Reservoir Rd., the completion of an overlay on New West Townsend Rd to the Townsend line, a section of
  13|                Lakefront Ave. beyond the scope of Emerald Place, and Chase Rd. (Rt. 13) from the State taking to just south of the
  14|                Northfield Rd. intersection. The scope of the project included: a leveling course of asphalt followed by a top or finish
  15|                course, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, minor drainage improvements and driveway aprons.
  16|                 Expanding the scope of our pavement preservation methods we added "crack sealing" to the overall program. This
  17|                 process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement. This
  18|                application can add approximately 5 years or more to the existing surface. The Pavement Management Plan also identifies
  19|                specific areas where this method is the best option.
  20|                Funding continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of
  21|                funding is the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 90 miles of
  22|                paved streets is, by industry standards, is calculated at 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the
  23|                operational budget remains stagnant. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of
  24|                funds is necessary. A conservative estimate would be approximately 1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional
  25|                $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000 a year to a backlog of work that exceeds 10 million dollars places
  26|                this department and the Town as a whole in dismal failure. Many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major
  27|                reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality. Patching, or filling pot holes by DPW staff is in most areas is no
  28|                longer the appropriate application. Without a substantial infusion of funds our roads Town-wide will continue to
  29|                deteriorate to a point where the safety of our travelling public will be compromised and the overall quality of is life
  30|                negatively affected.
  31|                Line Markings
  32|                For the first time in many years, adequate funding was made available to provide center line striping and fog lines at the
  33|                curb on all our major roadways. Further, where applicable, crosswalks, stop bars and designated parking areas were
  34|                repainted. By means of a supplemental allocation the Town has maintained compliance with current MUTCD mandates.
  35|                FLEET MAINTENANCE
  36|                The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance of all Town vehicles and equipment excluding the
  37|                 Fire Dept. apparatus. High performance police units, park, cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of
  38|                highway related machinery are serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become
  39|                 more frequent and therefore more costly. Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all petroleum
  40|                based products continues to be a moving target. Applying a solid budget number to gasoline and diesel fuel costs is
  41|                impossible.

   2|                   365 days per year.
   3|                 ENVIRONMENTAL
   4|                   Storm Water
   5|                   The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet set the requirements for Phase Il Storm water regulation and permits. As
   6|                   we await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
   7|                   The DPW is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990's earmarked
   8|                   $20,000.00 annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding
   9|                   placed the financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage and
  10|                   roadway repairs.
  11|                   As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed. Little or no
  12|                   preventative maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done due to a lack of available resources.
  13|                  Waste Water
  14|                  The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system from permitting, inspections, and daily preventative
  15|                   maintenance of collector lines and pumping stations to billing and documentation. There is but one full time person who
  16|                  diligently remains on call, 2417 365 for all sewer related emergencies.
  17|                  Landfill
  18|                  The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. is in compliance with the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored
  19|                  for excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  20|                  This year the frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of consistently favorable
  21|                  results. The perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  22|                   Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be hazardous
  23|                  waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit.
  24|                  The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials.
  25|                  On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush,
  26|                  leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a valuable component to our
  27|                  recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this
  28|                  location. Once again, dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it
  29|                  environmentally safe.
  30|                  Solid Waste
  31|                  Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and
  32|                  recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc.
  33|                  Lake Shirley Dam
  34|                  In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of
  35|                  the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record
  36|                  keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of
  37|                  the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency
  38|                  action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water
  39|                  program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with currept and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise

===PAGE 28=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                required to remain vigilant and on call, and at the ready throughout the winter season with no additional compensation.
   2|                PARKS
   3|                In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and grounds
   4|                exclusive of school property. A full time Park Superintendent was replaced with one seasonal employee. Much of the Town
   5|                wide landscape maintenance was outsourced resulting in a significant savings. The new multi-use track complex remains
   6|                under the control of the Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
   7|                CEMETERY
   8|               • One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South cemeteries,
   9|                maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission.
  10|                The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  11|                As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow Town employees as
  12|                we persevere through these difficult times.
  13|                "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of Lunenburg
  14|                for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of
  15|                the divisions under the umbrella of public works"
  16|                                                             SEWER COMMISSION
  17|               The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006. The Sewer
  18|                Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and
  19|                implementing rules, regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side
  20|                of the system. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary
  21|                sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager. During 2012, Dave MacDonald and Butch Bilotta were re-elected
  22|               for three year terms at the Town Election, in August our Chairman Bob Ebersole resigned, and Mike Nault was appointed in
  23|                November to finish out his term until the May 2013 elections.
  24|                There are currently 673 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 387 that have access to Town Sewer
  25|                but are not yet connected. During 2012, we had 35 new accounts connect to Town Sewer.
  26|                The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 80% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to
  27|               the City of Leominster, and the other 20% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. As the rates charged by the City of
  28|               Leominster and the City of Fitchburg remained stable during 2012, there were no rate increases to Lunenburg users since
  29|                1/1/12. However, in order to balance the FY 13 Budget, it was necessary for the Commission to transfer $73,208 from
  30|               retained earnings, a practice that is not sustainable. It is a goal of the Sewer Commission to present a balanced budget for
  31|             , the next fiscal year.
  32|               The Commission continues to focus on two major issues that affect the operating costs of the sewer system; grease and
  33|               infiltration and inflow (I/I). There has been excellent cooperation and progress made with several commercial
  34|               establishments and the Lunenburg School system related to grease trap design and maintenance. While the Grease Trap
  35|               Regulation, put into effect in 2011, focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual home owners can also contribute
  36|               a significant amount of grease into the system which increases everyone's costs. We will continue to educate homeowners
  37|               on how to protect the infrastructure and keep operating costs low by reducing their disposal of fats, oil and grease into the
  38|               system and avoiding blockages. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain. The second major
  39|                                                                       25

   3|                     The Commission is working to finalize a comprehensive review of all its governing bylaws, regulations, policies,

  30|                   Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.

   1|                The 2012 MCAS results were very positive and provided much to celebrate. Lunenburg continues to be ranked well at.
   2|                every grade level, and this year our grade 10 students' ELA performance was ranked #1 along with twenty-seven other high
   3|                performing schools in the Commonwealth with 99% of students scoring at proficient or above. Forty-one students received
   4|                Adams Scholarships. Student growth percentiles continue to be strong with exceptionally high growth noted in some
   5|                areas. There is clear evidence that our focus on continuous improvement and tiered instruction are positively impacting
   6|                student performance. We were also pleased to announce that the District was selected for the 3rd Annual Advanced
   7|                Placement (AP) District Honor Roll. A total of 539 school districts across the U.S. and Canada were honored by the College
   8|                Board for simultaneously increasing access to Advanced Placement® coursework while maintaining or increasing the
   9|                percentage of students earning scores of 3 or higher on AP Exams. Lunenburg was one of only forty-six districts in
  10|                Massachusetts to meet the criteria to be placed on the AP Honor Roll. Additionally, according to the College Board, more
  11|                school districts from Massachusetts earned a spot on the honor roll than any other state in the nation. We are very proud
  12|                of the work being done across the district to provide all students the opportunity to access rigorous curriculum.
  13|                I was pleased to recognize an outstanding member of the class of 2013, Molly Ellen O'Connell, as this year's
  14|                Superintendent's Award recipient. Eighth grader Ron Hyatt represented our schools and community at Governor Patrick's
  15|                Project 351 - 2013 day of service. These two students, along with a host of their peers across the district, continue to
  16|                demonstrate the vitality of our Mission - to prepare students for responsible community membership, Students across the
  17|                district continue to collect for the town food bank, as well as raise awareness and funds for important causes. Of particular
  18|                note this year are the efforts of winter track athletes who fundraised for a fellow student's education and who were also
  19|                awarded a sportsmanship award by Massachusetts Interscholastic Athletic Association. Additionally, with the support of
  20|                the Lunenburg PTO, Lunenburg Fire Department, Lunenburg Water Department, and numerous volunteers, the community
  21|                ice rink was constructed. The rink was envisioned and planned by high school teachers Mr. Robert Truax and Mr. Joshua
  22|                Koziol and students in their algebra class the previous year. Ours is indeed a caring community of learners working hard
  23|                each day, learning and growing and giving back to the community that has supported them and their education.
  24|                The Thomas C. Passios School continues to serve an important role hosting a variety of programs for students, as well as
  25|                school and community services and events. The district's Extended Day, Early Learning, and School Linked Services
  26|                programs operate in the building, as does a FLLAC collaborative day program that serves twenty-nine students from area
  27|                schools including three from Lunenburg. In addition to the district offices, Lunenburg Cable Access has a studio at the
  28|                school. A classroom in the school has been converted to a meeting room and professional development center in the
  29|                building. Numerous organizations utilize the building for meetings and events including the Lunenburg PTO, Musicaiders,
  30|                Scouts, and Junior Basketball. The Brooks House also continues to serve the district and community as well, housing the
  31|                facilities office, a meeting room, and the local newspaper.
  32|                This past year our schools bid farewell to four retirees who devoted many years to public service-their total tenure in
  33|               • Lunenburg represents 60 years of service. Retiring this past year were Susan Glenny, school nurse (19 years); Al Carlson,
  34|                maintenance (18 years); Jennifer Hood Biker, grade 8 teacher (16 years); and Christine Bonci, principal (7 years). We
  35|                deeply appreciate the many contributions of these dedicated and committed professionals and extend our best wishes to
  36|                them in their retirement.
  37|                This past year we also were deeply saddened by the passing of Elisabeth Estrada-Turner who had served the students of
  38|                the district the past eight years as a tutor. We were also deeply impacted by the senseless loss of 20 young children and 6
  39|                dedicated educators in Newtown, CT. The students, educators, and citizens of Lunenburg responded with an outpouring of
  40|                love and concern in remembrance and in response to these tragic losses. We will always keep them and their families our

   5|                                                         LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   6|                                                         Elaine E. Blaisdell, Interim Principal
   7|                 The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality early childhood education for all
   8|                 students. We are a community of teachers, students, and parents working together to create a safe and respectful
   9|                 environment for children. Our current knowledge of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and
  10|                 provides the foundation for student learning.
  11|                 This year the school serviced preschool to third grade students after the closing of the Thomas C. Passios Elementary
  12|                 School. We started the 2012-2013 school year with 506 students in 20 classrooms for an average class size of 23, and 41
  13|                 students in our two preschool classrooms. We constantly strive to find better ways to meet the collective as well as the
  14|                 individual needs of our students. Our Professional Development focused on Reading Comprehension, the Common Core
  15|                 curriculum, and the new teacher evaluation system. We worked with experts in the field of Literacy and established
  16|                 eighteen lab classrooms in the building that are piloting Readers Workshop and Inclusive Practices. All of our classroom
  17|                 teachers continued to use Responsive Classroom strategies    as part of our Academic/Social/Emotional Learning
  18|                 Curriculum, which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and logical consequences, and academic choice.
  19|                 The Primary School community continues to strive toward academic excellence. Common assessments are in place in the
  20|                fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common
  21|                assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive instruction. We are now able to track
  22|                progress throughout the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve instruction.
  23|                The Primary School is a Title One school; we have continued our tiered instructional system to insure that all students
  24|                have the services they need to be successful. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language Arts and
  25|                Math. The first tier is the largest, and is fitted for universal instruction in the classroom. Teachers are skilled in
  26|                differentiating instruction to meet a large number of student needs in the classroom. The second tier is based on student
  27|                needs that are targeted, individual, and involve more intensive interventions. The third tier is the smallest and involves
  28|                special services with very intense interventions. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the
  29|                needs of the students who may be struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to strive to
  30|                achieve higher levels of learning. Learning is becoming more personalized and students are being challenged to be
  31|                independent learners and problem solvers.
  32|                Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture that is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our members. We
  33|                have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where
  34|                members feel a sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment that
  35|                is safe, promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
  36|                Student Success Night was held for all Kindergarten, Grade 1 and 2 students and their families in the spring of 2012. This
  37|                night serves as the vehicle for students to display/articulate their strengths as a learner. To prepare for Student Success
  38|                Night, students worked with their classroom teachers to identify and articulate their strengths in four areas: academics,
  39|                habits of mind, creativity, and citizenship. Examples of student work in these areas were displayed in the classrooms.
  40|                Students acted as tour guides for their families on Success Night.
  41|                The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school issues, review the annual budget for the school and
  42|                develop the School Improvement Plan, as well as make revisions to the school's handbook.
  43|                Members of the Council for the 2012 -2013 school year were: 31

   4|                  Turkey Hill Middle School for the third straight year is a Great Books Foundation Model School, based on our excellence in
   5|                 instruction and ability to provide meaningful opportunities for the development of critical thinking skills. For the past three
   6|                 years our faculty has been collaborating with the Great Books program experts on how to refine our instruction and
   7|                 improve writing skills throughout the curriculum. The feedback provided by the model school experts is positive and THMS
   8|                 continues to excel in the targeted areas of writing and critical thinking when compared to other model schools across the
   9|                 country. This year, Turkey Hill Middle School will host a "Learning Walk" in which area educators from throughout the state
  10|                 will come to THMS to observe and learn how to implement the successful strategies that our teachers are currently using.
  11|                 Our teaching staff will also be videotaped in order to be used as a training tool for other aspiring educators.

  13|                 lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success are our talented faculty
  14|                 who are committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and

   9|                                                          LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
  10|                                                            Brian Spadafino, Principal
  11|                Lunenburg High School was a hub for academic and social activity in 2012-2013. Even before school started, there was a
  12|                lot of change swirling around LHS. We have a new administrative team in place and, as a result of the district-wide
  13|                consolidation, we welcomed nine members to the LHS staff. We also hired two teachers to the LHS faculty and the district. *
  14|                A transition team of five teachers met this summer to plan ways to ensure that student and staff needs would be met. With
  15|                the assistance of 44 student leaders, we successfully transitioned almost 250 eighth and ninth grade students into the LHS
  16|               culture by having an eighth and ninth grade orientation on the first day of school. We opened the year, as we do every
  17|               year, with a guest speaker from the Worcester County District Attorney's Office to discuss responsible Internet use,
  18|                Facebook, cyber-bullying and Internet safety with our incoming students and parents.
  19|                NEASC ACCREDITATION
  20|               Lunenburg High School recently completed the Two Year Progress Report for the Commission on Public Secondary
  21|               Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges. In 2010, the Commission recommended that the school
  22|               receive continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based upon review of an evaluation
  23|               report prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010.
  24|               While the Commission lauded LHS in many areas, the Commission warned LHS in three areas. Of major concern were
  25|              facility and technology issues. The Commission stated that the physical plant significantly limits the school's ability to
  26|               meet the educational needs of students, and that there is an expectation to develop both a short- and long-term plan to
  27|               fund and address the significant facilities concerns including infrastructure and wiring issues, roof and window issues,
  28|               limited handicap accessibility, and inadequate space. In curriculum, the commission recommended the further
  29|               development of a standard template to clearly articulate school-wide expectations for student learning.
  30|               As part of the Two Year Progress Report, a committee was assembled to address the recommendations that were made by
  31|               the NEASC visiting committee. Three teachers and two administrators went through the NEASC report and needed to
  32|               address whether the recommendations were: completed, in progress, or planned for the future. The Commission identified
  33|               nine Highlighted recommendations. Of the Highlighted recommendations, two were deemed "complete" and seven were
  34|               deemed "in progress." One of the major recommendations that needed to be addressed was the issue of the LHS facility.
  35|              Twenty-seven other recommendations were also addressed in the Two Year Progress Report. The report was submitted to the Commission on January 1, 2013.
  36|                                                                     35

   1|                                                                  SCHOOL FACILITIES
   2|                                                              John Londa, Facilities Director
   3|                    Planning for renovations or replacement of Lunenburg High School, or the development of a middle school / high school
   4|                    building, continued to move forward with the approval at the May 2012 Annual Town Meeting of $750,000 for the feasibility
   5|                    study. Between July and September, the School Building Committee approved a request for qualifications for an owner's
   6|                    project manager and interviewed qualified candidates. In October 2012, Joslin, Lesser + Associates was selected as the
   7|                    owner's project manager for the project at a cost of $125,000. Between December 2012 and February 2013, a request for
   8|                    qualifications for a designer was released, architectural firms submitted their qualifications, and the selection board
   9|                   selected three finalists to interview. On February 5, 2013, the Massachusetts School Building Authority designer selection panel designated Tappé Associates as the top-ranked finalist.
  10|                   Due to significant restraints on the School Department operational budget in FY13, the School Committee at the end of the
  11|                   prior school year approved the consolidation of the district to three school buildings. Lunenburg Primary School was
  12|                   configured to grades Pre-K to 3, the Turkey Hill Middle School to grades 4-7, and Lunenburg High School to grades 8-12.
  13|                   The Thomas C. Passios Elementary School became home to the Caldwell School, a special services school of the FLLAC
  14|                   Collaborative, which rented roughly 1/3 of the space in the school. The remainder of the space was used by the district's
  15|                   extended daycare program, the district administration, and Lunenburg Public Access Cable. The consolidation of the
  16|                   schools was completed in July and August, with the custodial and maintenance staff relocating teacher materials and some classroom furniture.
  17|                   The FY13 capital plan approved the re-seaming of the existing 25-year-old rubber membrane roof on the Thomas C. Passios
  18|                   Elementary School in order to extend the life of the roof. Haynes, Leineck, and Smith, Inc. was the project designer at a
  19|                   cost of $9,098. The project was completed by M.D.M. Engineering in November 2012 at a cost of $73,000. Snow guards
  20|                   were added to the Turkey Hill Middle School gym to safeguard against snow loads falling from the gym roof and causing
  21|                   injury to someone, and in order to protect lower elevation roofs. The Passios roof work and the Middle School snow guard
  22|                  work were bid together with M.D.M. Engineering completing the snow guard work at a cost of $18,010.
  23|                  The focus of the school maintenance effort in light of the FY13 budget is to ensure that all school buildings continue to
  24|                   support education by ensuring as rapid a response as possible is made to complete work orders for repairs, to continue to
  25|                  ensure a preventative maintenance program, and when possible, security and minor improvements to energy efficiency.
  26|                  Work to improve the appearance to spaces such as carpet replacement, painting, replacement of furniture, upgrades to
  27|                  the first seven months of the year, 437 work orders were completed. fixtures and other new work is currently not possible without sacrificing preventative maintenance or support for repairs: In
  28|                  For the FY14 Capital Program, the School Department requested $13,800 for each of three school buildings to upgrade the
  29|                  front end controller to the HVAC control systems as its highest priority, $15,000 for asbestos remediation at three schools,
  30|                  and $16,000 to replace flooring in the Lunenburg Primary School cafeteria and teacher lunch room. Armstrong Flooring,
  31|                 Inc. has agreed to assist by providing materials for the replacement of the linoleum with vinyl composition tile. Replacement of the floor is needed due to water vapor causing the linoleum to bubble.
  32|                                                                         38

===PAGE 42=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                 A Monty Tech education is grounded in workforce preparedness, balanced by a rigorous academic curriculum. Our
   2|                 year Monty Tech conducts a One-Year-Out Survey, measuring "positive placements," as defined by the Carl D. Perkins
   3|               22222 Vocational and Technical Education Act. By tracking students who have enrolled in post-secondary education programs,
   4|                 secured employment aligned to their vocational training, or are serving our country in the United States military, we are
   5|                 better able to inform potential students, advise current students, and deliver vocational-technical programs that are
   6|                relevant to today's workforce trends and labor market needs. One year after leaving Monty Tech, the graduates of 2011
   7|                reported the following:
   8|                         33% are currently employed in a field related to their Monty Tech trade
   9|                         12% are employed in a field unrelated to their vocational education
  10|                         45% are currently pursuing a higher education, and
  11|                       8% have elected to serve in the United States military
  12|                Compiling information for the Annual Report has provided me with an opportunity to reflect on the achievements and
  13|                activities of our students, faculty and staff. I am fortunate to work closely with a talented leadership team, many of whom
  14|                have contributed to this report, and given great insight into some of the more notable highlights of the 2011-2012 academic
  15|                year, including:
  16|                • 95% of our students scored Advanced or Proficient in the state's English Language Arts exam, up from 87% in 2010-
  17|                    2011.
  18|                • 88% of our students scored Advanced or Proficient in the state's mathematics exam, up from 87% in 2010-2011.
  19|                • Class of 2012 graduate Victoria Holbert served as the National SkillsUSA President, traveling across the country and
  20|                    abroad to advocate for vocational-technical education. She participated in the "Education Nation Summit," engaging
  21|                    in high-level discussions on the state of the American Education system.
  22|                • Seven students advanced through local, district and state competitions to earn the right to compete at the National
  23|                    SkillsUSA Conference in Kansas City, Missouri. The Monty Tech national delegation was awarded four gold medals
  24|                    and two bronze medals.
  25|                • For the third consecutive year, the Monty Tech Marine Corps JROTC program was awarded the distinction of "Naval
  26|                    Honor School," in recognition of exemplary community service.
  27|                • One outstanding JROTC Cadet was awarded the Naval Reserve Officers Training Corps Scholarship, receiving full
  28|                    tuition, fees, books, uniforms, summer experiences, as well as monthly stipends. She is now attending Norwich
  29|                    University.
  30|                • Monty Tech continues to participate in the highly selective Student Spaceflight Experiment Program, representing the
  31|                    only vocational school in the nation to have a student science experiment launch into space, and providing students
  32|                    with an opportunity to study the effects of gravity on their science project.
  33|                Much like districts across the state, Monty Tech has been affected by the nationwide economic recession. Maintaining
  34|                high standards of fiscal responsibility, with an eye on our over-arching mission of increasing student achievement, this
  35|                school has been able to maintain our high standards of academic success, while presenting a budget that is fiscally
  36|                conservative. Our FY12 Annual Budget was unanimously accepted by each of the eighteen member communities, and
  37|               reflected a 2.4% increase over the 2010-2011 Educational Plan. We deeply appreciate the public's support for Monty Tech.
  38|                We recognize that we are in the midst of some of the most difficult economic times faced by residents of our member cities
  39|                and towns. As a result, we will continue to strive to seek creative ways to keep down costs to our 18 member communities.

   7|                                                                  Leadership
   8|                 The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented administrators
   9|                 whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise contribute to the success of
  10|                 the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the Superintendent and Principal, the team has been
  11|                 able to transform the school into one of the most sought-after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  12|                 Nicholas DeSimone, Principal                               Richard Nutt, Director of Vocational Programs

  18|                                                                   Enrollment
  19|                 On October 1, 2011, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,435 students in grades nine through twelve. Students
  20|                 are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (58), Ashby (37), Athol (110), Barre (39), Fitchburg
  21|                 (376), Gardner (173), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (56), Lunenburg (72), Petersham (8), Phillipston (17),
  22|                 Princeton (14), Royalston (24), Sterling (58), Templeton (110), Westminster (70), and Winchendon (127).
  23|                 Throughout 2011-2012, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community members to
  24|                 learn about and visit the school. In October 2011, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders participated in the annual
  25|                 "Tour Day" event. Students toured our twenty vocational/technical areas and learned about the school's challenging
  26|                 academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested
  27|                 students the opportunity to return in the evening with their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with
  28|                 staff members.
  29|                 Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  30|                 admissions. 2011-2012 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for him, as the school received a record 823
  31|                 applications for admission. Of those, 773 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class. The
  32|                 balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because there are only
  33|                 a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 380 freshmen and 24 upperclassmen were admitted.
  34|                  The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit Monty Tech
  35|                  after school, and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of vocational/technical areas. The
  36|                  program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2011-2012 school year, serving approximately 675
  37|                  area students.
